# The Church Orders in the Sehling Corpus: An Inventory by Tradition

This guide lists every order that Sehling's *Die evangelischen Kirchenordnungen des XVI.
Jahrhunderts* prints, volume by volume and territory by territory: 2,581 texts in 24 volumes (30
parts). For each order it gives:
- its number in Sehling, its title, and its date;
- the page in Sehling and the `eko.db` document that holds the text;
- what kind of order it is (church order, agenda, visitation order, mandate, and so on);
- the confessional tradition it belongs to: Lutheran, Reformed, Moderate Reformed (Bucer's
  circle and the Upper German cities), Philippist, Anglican, Hussite, and a few others;
- whether it is an Interim order, or otherwise tied to the Augsburg Interim of 1548.

**Layout**

- §1 summarizes the findings.
- §2 sets out the scope, the method and the cautions.
- §3 defines the traditions and says where each is found.
- §4 lists the Interim orders and the other texts tied to the Interim.
- §5 is a table of the territories at a glance.
- §§6-35 are the inventory itself, one section per volume. Each territory has a short note on
  its confessional history, followed by a table of its orders.

**Related guides.** The topical guides listed in `README.md` mark every order by this
inventory's traditions (§3) and Interim orders (§4).

**Conventions**

- **Titles** are Sehling's, as his tables of contents give them. For volumes 1-5 they come from
  the chronological register at the end of each volume. Long titles are shortened, and "…" marks
  the cut. Spelling is as printed, including OCR errors.
- **No.** is the number Sehling (or the later editor) gives the text within its territory.
  Volumes 1-5 number their registers in their own way, and their numbers are given as they
  stand.
- **Date** is the year in the title or in the register. "n.d." means none is given.
- **Sehling** gives the volume and the page where the text begins (for volume 14, the
  introduction page where Sehling only describes the order).
- **Doc** is the `eko.db` document that holds that page.
- **Kind** is assigned from the words of the title (§2.4).
- **Trad.** uses the codes of §3:
  - **L** = Lutheran;
  - **R** = Reformed;
  - **MR** = Moderate Reformed (Bucerian and Upper German);
  - **PH** = Philippist;
  - **A** = Anglican;
  - **H** = Hussite (Unity of the Bohemian Brethren);
  - **C** = Sandomierz consensus (Lutheran, Reformed and Brethren together);
  - **R/L** = Reformed and Lutheran (treaty between the two);
  - **RCH** = Roman Catholic humanist (Erasmian reform);
  - **RC** = Roman Catholic;
  - **RAD** = Radical Reformation (Müntzer);
  - **ANA** = Anabaptist (civic toleration of the Anabaptists);
  - **T** = Multi-confessional diet decree (Catholic, Lutheran, Reformed and Unitarian received
    side by side).
- Block quotations are Sehling's or his successors' own words, followed by an English
  translation.

## 1. Summary of findings

**1. The set holds 2,581 orders.** Sehling took "church order" in the widest sense (§2.1). The
set therefore includes far more than the great church orders and agendas:

| Kind | Texts |
|---|---|
| Church order | 333 |
| Agenda / liturgy | 271 |
| Visitation | 346 |
| Mandate / decree | 436 |
| Consistory / synod / government | 265 |
| Discipline / police | 178 |
| Marriage | 90 |
| Poor relief | 82 |
| School / university | 65 |
| Catechism / instruction | 44 |
| Monastery / chapter | 46 |
| Appointment / oath | 59 |
| Treaty / agreement | 77 |
| Articles / statutes | 45 |
| Local order | 59 |
| Confession / doctrine | 31 |
| Property / endowment | 6 |
| Other | 148 |

The full church orders (**333**) and the agendas and other liturgical orders (**271**) together
make up about a quarter of the set. Many of the rest are visitation orders, mandates, consistory
and synod orders, and orders on marriage, discipline, schools and the poor.

**2. Most of the orders are Lutheran.** By tradition:

| Tradition | Code | Texts |
|---|---|---|
| Lutheran | L | 1953 |
| Reformed | R | 336 |
| Moderate Reformed (Bucerian and Upper German) | MR | 199 |
| Philippist | PH | 33 |
| Anglican | A | 1 |
| Hussite (Unity of the Bohemian Brethren) | H | 3 |
| Sandomierz consensus (Lutheran, Reformed and Brethren together) | C | 6 |
| Reformed and Lutheran (treaty between the two) | R/L | 2 |
| Roman Catholic humanist (Erasmian reform) | RCH | 18 |
| Roman Catholic | RC | 1 |
| Radical Reformation (Müntzer) | RAD | 3 |
| Anabaptist (civic toleration of the Anabaptists) | ANA | 1 |
| Multi-confessional diet decree (Catholic, Lutheran, Reformed and Unitarian received side by side) | T | 25 |

- **Lutheran** (1,953): Saxony, Brandenburg, Prussia, Pomerania, the Baltic, Mecklenburg, the
  Hanse towns, Lower Saxony, Franconia, Württemberg, Hohenlohe and most of the imperial cities
  after 1552.
- **Reformed** (336): above all the Electoral Palatinate (from 1561/63) and the "Second
  Reformation" counties of the Wetterau and the Rhine (Nassau-Dillenburg, Hanau-Münzenberg,
  Ysenburg, Solms-Braunfels, Sayn, Wied, Wittgenstein, Moers, Bentheim-Tecklenburg),
  Pfalz-Zweibrücken (1588), Pfalz-Simmern (1598, under the Electoral Palatinate), Hessen-Kassel
  (1600/1605), Lippe (1600), Anhalt (1596), Bremen (from 1580), Emden and the East Frisian
  Reformed congregations, the London Dutch church, Mülhausen (Mulhouse), and the French refugee
  churches of Frankfurt, Wetzlar and the Lebertal.
- **Moderate Reformed** (199): Bucer's Strasbourg and the Upper German cities before the Interim
  (Konstanz, Ulm, Esslingen, Augsburg, Memmingen, Lindau, Isny, Biberach, Gengenbach, Landau,
  Worms, early Frankfurt); Philip's Hesse to 1566; Hermann von Wied's Cologne Reformation
  (1543-1546); Rothmann's Münster (1532/33); East Frisia under Countess Anna; Colmar after 1578,
  whose pastors came from Basel.
- **Philippist** (33): the joint Hessian synods and orders of 1568-1582 and the Hessian orders
  that followed them to 1598; Bremen (1556-1575); Nassau-Dillenburg (1575); Anhalt (1590-1594).
- **Anglican** (1): the order of the English exile congregation at Frankfurt, the "Old
  Discipline" and "New Discipline" of 1557 (§3.4).
- **Hussite** (3): the order of the Unity of the Bohemian Brethren (synod of Zerawitz, 1616) and
  the privileges for the Brethren's congregations at Ostroróg (1569) and Lissa (1580), in
  Greater Poland (§3.5). Six more Polish texts belong to the **Sandomierz consensus** of 1570,
  in which Lutherans, Reformed and Brethren joined.
- **Others**, each labelled with its own affiliation (§3.6):
  - **Roman Catholic humanist (Erasmian)** (18): the church orders of Jülich-Cleves-Berg;
  - **Roman Catholic** (1): the Bishop of Münster's edict against the Anabaptists (1534);
  - **Radical Reformation** (3): Thomas Müntzer's Allstedt orders;
  - **Anabaptist** (1): the Münster council's mandate of January 1534 that let each keep his
    faith and so recognized the Anabaptists;
  - **Multi-confessional** (25): the decrees of the Transylvanian diets;
  - **Reformed and Lutheran** (2): two East Frisian treaties between the Lutheran count and
    Reformed Emden.
- **Not in the set**: no order of the Waldensians, Lollards, Utraquists, Schwenckfelders,
  Hutterites, Mennonites or Antitrinitarians. Anabaptists, Schwenckfelders, Davidjorists,
  Mennonites and "Sakramentierer" appear only as the targets of some thirty mandates, which are
  coded by the authority that issued them.

**3. Almost no Interim orders.** Sehling deliberately left out the orders made under the
Augsburg Interim of 1548 (§2.2). Only four Interim orders proper are in the set (§4):
- the Brandenburg-Ansbach "Mehrung der vorigen kirchenordnung" (the "Auctuarium") of 1548,
  printed "because of its special importance";
- the East Frisian Interim order of 16 July 1549, printed in the editor's introduction;
- the Württemberg Interim mandate of 1548;
- the Biberach council's mandate after accepting the Interim (1548).

16 further texts are tied to the Interim in other ways: issued under it, about it, or ending it
(§4).

**4. Tradition followed the ruler, and changed with him.** Many territories changed tradition
within the century, and their orders change with them:
- **Electoral Palatinate**: Lutheran → Reformed (1561/63) → Lutheran (1577) → Reformed (1583).
- **Upper Palatinate**: Lutheran → Reformed (1559) → Lutheran (1577) → Reformed (1583).
- **Nassau-Dillenburg**: Lutheran → Philippist (1573) → Reformed (1578).
- **Bremen**: Lutheran → Philippist (1562) → Reformed (1580).
- **Hanau-Münzenberg** (1595), **Sayn** (1605), **Lippe** (1600), **Pfalz-Zweibrücken** (1588),
  **Wittgenstein** (about 1574), **Moers** (1578) and **Bentheim-Tecklenburg** (1580s): Lutheran
  → Reformed.
- **Ysenburg**: Ronneburg-Kelsterbach was Reformed 1584-1597, then Lutheran again; Birstein
  became Reformed in 1596.
- **The Upper German cities**: Upper German before the Interim, Lutheran afterwards.

---

## 2. Scope, sources and cautions

### 2.1 What Sehling counted as a church order

Sehling took the term in its widest sense, to cover every ordinance that regulated church
affairs. **Sehling, preface to vol. 1, 1902** (Sehling 1, p. VIII):

<!-- doc 1 -->
> Wir fassen daher für unsere Sammlung den Begriff „Kirchen-Ordnung“im weitesten Sinne des
> Wortes und verstehen darunter alle zur Regelung der kirchlichen Verhältnisse bestimmten
> Verfügungen (mit Ausnahme natürlich der nur vorübergehenden Einzelzwecken dienenden
> Anordnungen).

We therefore take the term "church order" for our collection in the widest sense of the word,
and understand by it all ordinances made for the regulation of church affairs (excepting, of
course, those orders that served merely passing and particular ends).

### 2.2 What he left out: half measures and the Interim

Sehling also excluded orders that were never in force, and the "half measures" of wavering
rulers. **Sehling, preface to vol. 1, 1902** (Sehling 1, p. XV):

<!-- doc 1 -->
> Ferner werden nur die wahrhaft evangelischen Kirchen-Ordnungen gebracht werden. Ausgenommen
> sind die halben Massregeln, wie sie schwankende Obrigkeiten zur Beschwichtigung der Gemüther,
> oder wohl auch im Bestreben, Reformen einzuführen, getroffen haben, ohne dass es jedoch
> eigentlich evangelische Ordnungen geworden wären. Insonderheit gedenke ich nicht abzudrucken
> die Interims-Kirchen-Ordnungen.

Furthermore, only the truly evangelical church orders will be given. Excepted are the half
measures that wavering authorities made to calm men's minds, or perhaps in an effort to bring in
reforms, without their ever becoming truly evangelical orders. In particular I do not intend to
print the Interim church orders.

He held to this for Saxony, where the great Interim agenda of 1549 is only described. **Sehling,
introduction to Albertine Saxony** (Sehling 1, p. 102):

<!-- doc 7 -->
> Die Zeit des Interims wird in unserer Sammlung nur gestreift, und es wird daher auch die in
> diese Zeit fallende grosse Ordnung, die, ein Produkt der Noth und der Nachgiebigkeit, nicht
> als eine rein evangelische Ordnung bezeichnet werden kann, hier nicht weiter behandelt.

The period of the Interim is only touched on in our collection, and so the great order that
falls in that period is not treated further here either: a product of necessity and of yielding,
it cannot be called a purely evangelical order.

His successors kept the rule, with one deliberate exception. **Brandenburg-Ansbach-Kulmbach,
editor's introduction** (Sehling 11, p. 291):

<!-- doc 274 -->
> Obwohl im allgemeinen Interimsordnungen in diese Ausgabe nicht aufgenommen werden sollen, wird
> diese ihrer besonderen Bedeutung wegen doch als Nr. IV 4 zum Abdruck gebracht.

Although Interim orders are in general not to be taken into this edition, this one is
nevertheless printed as no. IV 4 because of its special importance.

Later volumes also print Reformed, Erasmian and multi-confessional orders: the Reformed
Palatinate and the Wetterau counties, the Jülich orders, the Transylvanian diets. The inventory
follows the volumes as they stand.

### 2.3 How the list was made

- **The list of texts.** It is taken from each volume's own table of contents (volumes 6-24) or
  chronological register (volumes 1-5).
  - Where the scanned contents page is unusable, the titles were read from the running heads of
    the texts. This applies to Oldenburg and Jever in vol. 7/2.1.
  - The registers of volumes 1-5 list orders printed "ganz oder theilweise", in whole or in
    part. They therefore include some orders printed only in extract within an introduction.
  - Volume 14 (Kurpfalz) lists, among its texts, some orders that Sehling only describes in his
    introduction. These are marked "described in the introduction only".
  - Introductions, registers and editorial chapters are left out.
- **Tradition.** It is assigned by territory and date, following the editors' introductions.
  Each territory's note in §§6-35 gives its confessional history and the dates of change; each
  order takes the tradition of its territory at its date. Where an order belongs to a group with
  another tradition, it is marked individually. Examples:
  - a refugee congregation in a Lutheran city;
  - Hessen-Darmstadt orders within divided Hesse;
  - Solms-Braunfels within Solms;
  - the Lemgo recess of 1617, which guaranteed the town's Lutheran confession within Reformed
    Lippe.
- **Interim.** Texts are marked from their titles and from the editors' introductions (§4).

### 2.4 Kinds

The **Kind** column sorts the orders by the main word of the title. The kinds are:
- *Church order*: Kirchenordnung, Agenda das ist Kirchenordnung, Reformation, ordinatio;
- *Agenda / liturgy*: Agende, Messe, Gottesdienst, Taufe, Trauung, Abendmahl, Gesang, Gebet,
  Litanei, Vesper, Beichte, Busse, Ordination;
- *Visitation*: visitation orders and recesses, general articles;
- *Consistory / synod / government*: consistory, synod, church council, superintendent, classis,
  presbytery, ministerium, church officers;
- *Marriage*, *School / university*, *Poor relief*, *Monastery / chapter*, *Appointment / oath*,
  *Confession / doctrine*, *Catechism / instruction*, *Treaty / agreement*, *Property /
  endowment*;
- *Articles / statutes*: articles, instructions, leges, statutes;
- *Discipline / police*: Zucht, Polizei, Sonntag, Feiertag, Täufer, Landordnung;
- *Mandate / decree*: Mandat, Edikt, Befehl, Ausschreiben, Verordnung;
- *Local order*: an "Ordnung für" a town or village without further description;
- *Other*.

Large church orders contain agendas, catechisms and much else besides. The Kind column gives
only the leading character of the title. Search the text in `eko.db` for the parts of a large
order.

### 2.5 Cautions

- **Coding by territory.** The tradition codes follow the territory and its ruler. They do not
  judge each text's theology; a Lutheran territory's marriage or poor-law order is coded L.
- **Moderate Reformed and Philippist.** The orders that stood between the Lutheran and the
  Reformed are divided into two groups (§3.3):
  - **Moderate Reformed**: Bucer's Strasbourg and the Upper German Zwinglian-Bucerian cities;
    Philip of Hesse's church, which stood within the Wittenberg Concord but bore Bucer's stamp;
    Hermann von Wied's Cologne order; and the other orders of Bucer's sphere;
  - **Philippist**: the churches that held to Melanchthon's later teaching and his *Corpus
    doctrinae* and would not accept the Formula of Concord: the joint Hessian synods, Bremen,
    Nassau-Dillenburg and Anhalt, each on its way to the Reformed.

  Where an order could be placed in either group, it is counted as Moderate Reformed. Use the
  territory notes for the finer distinctions.

- **Early orders** (1522-1530) come before the confessional lines were fixed. They are coded by
  what the territory became; Müntzer's orders are coded as radical.
- **Pages and dates.** Some come from OCR of contents pages and registers. Where a register's
  page was plainly wrong, it was checked against the text; a few may still be out by a page or
  two.
- **Doc** is the `eko.db` document holding the start page. One database record may hold several
  orders, and one order may run over several records.

---

## 3. The traditions

### 3.1 Lutheran (L)

Orders of territories and cities that held to the Augsburg Confession in its Lutheran reading,
from the Wittenberg orders of the 1520s to the Formula of Concord and after. 1,953 texts.
Volumes 3, 5, 6/1, 6/2, 7/2.1, 11, 15, 16 and 23 are wholly Lutheran, and volume 1 is too, save
Müntzer's three orders. Lutheran orders are a minority only in volumes 8 (Hesse, coded Moderate
Reformed and Philippist), 10 (Nassau, Hanau, Ysenburg), 14 and 19/2 (Kurpfalz), 20/1
(Strasbourg) and 20/2 (Alsace).

### 3.2 Reformed (R)

Orders of churches that took the Reformed (Swiss, Calvinist or Heidelberg) confession. Most
belong to the "Second Reformation" of German territories that turned from Lutheran to Reformed
after 1560. The rest are refugee and stranger churches (French, Dutch, Walloon) and the
Swiss-allied city of Mülhausen.

- Principality of Anhalt (Sehling 2, §7): 3 texts, 1596-1599.
- Greater Poland (Posen): synods, the Bohemian Brethren and the towns (Sehling 4, §9): 3 texts,
  1560-1561.
- East Frisia: Reformed orders of limited range (Norden, Emden, Leer, Lütetsburg; London)
  (Sehling 7/1, §13): 15 texts, 1528-1606.
- City of Bremen (Sehling 7/2.2, §15): 13 texts, 1582-1616.
- Divided Hesse (1582-1618) (Sehling 9, §17): 11 texts, 1600-1618.
- County of Solms (Sehling 9, §17): 7 texts, 1582-1616.
- Imperial city of Frankfurt (Sehling 9, §17): 2 texts, 1554-1585.
- Imperial city of Wetzlar (Sehling 9, §17): 2 texts, 1586-1596.
- Nassau-Dillenburg (Sehling 10, §18): 14 texts, 1581-1618.
- County of Hanau-Münzenberg (Sehling 10, §18): 24 texts, 1596-1614.
- County of Ysenburg (Sehling 10, §18): 12 texts, 1580-1614.
- Upper Palatinate (Electoral Palatinate) (Sehling 13, §21): 13 texts, 1566-1615.
- Electoral Palatinate (Kurpfalz) (Sehling 14, §22): 67 texts, 1561-1615.
- Duchy of Pfalz-Zweibrücken (Sehling 18, §27): 13 texts, 1588-1617.
- County of Sayn (Sehling 19/1, §28): 3 texts, 1606-1619.
- County of Wied (Sehling 19/1, §28): 8 texts, 1564-1619.
- Electoral Palatinate (supplement to vol. 14) (Sehling 19/2, §29): 61 texts, 1585-1610.
- Principality of Pfalz-Simmern (Sehling 19/2, §29): 2 texts, 1598.
- Lordship of Rappoltstein (Sehling 20/2, §31): 10 texts, 1558-1569.
- Imperial city of Mülhausen (Mulhouse) (Sehling 20/2, §31): 27 texts, 1523-1582.
- County of Lippe (Sehling 21, §32): 1 text, 1600.
- Archbishopric of Cologne (Sehling 22, §33): 2 texts, 1583.
- County of Wittgenstein (Sehling 22, §33): 10 texts, 1579-1611.
- County of Moers (Sehling 22, §33): 3 texts, 1581-1603.
- County of Bentheim-Tecklenburg (Sehling 22, §33): 10 texts, 1601-1619.

### 3.3 Moderate Reformed (MR) and Philippist (PH)

Between the Lutheran and the Swiss Reformed stood two groups of orders. They are coded apart,
and where an order could belong to either, it is counted as Moderate Reformed.

**Moderate Reformed (MR)**, 199 texts: the Bucerian and Upper German orders, and others close to
them:
- the Upper German Reformation of Bucer, Capito, Blarer and Zwick in the southern imperial
  cities before the Interim;
- Philip of Hesse's church, which bore Bucer's stamp. Bucer had a decisive share in the orders
  of 1539, and Philip's testament gave the Wittenberg Concord, Bucer's work, almost confessional
  weight. The editors place the Kirchenordnung of 1566 in the Strasbourg sphere;
- Hermann von Wied's Cologne order (written by Bucer and Melanchthon);
- a few early evangelical orders that moved toward Zurich (Rothmann's Münster) or took their
  pastors from Basel and ended Reformed (Colmar after 1578), and East Frisia under Countess Anna
  and John a Lasco.

- East Frisia: comital orders (Sehling 7/1, §13): 1 text, 1545.
- Landgraviate of Hesse under Philip (to 1567) (Sehling 8, §16): 21 texts, 1526-1566.
- Imperial city of Frankfurt (Sehling 9, §17): 4 texts, 1530-1533.
- Imperial city of Augsburg (Sehling 12, §20): 14 texts, 1523-1548.
- Imperial city of Lindau (Sehling 12, §20): 3 texts, 1533-1539.
- Imperial city of Memmingen (Sehling 12, §20): 4 texts, 1528-1542.
- Imperial city of Konstanz (Sehling 17/1, §25): 19 texts, 1524-1548.
- Imperial city of Isny (Sehling 17/1, §25): 2 texts, 1533-1544.
- Imperial city of Gengenbach (Sehling 17/1, §25): 8 texts, 1538-1545.
- Imperial city of Ulm (Sehling 17/2, §26): 15 texts, 1528-1544.
- Imperial city of Esslingen (Sehling 17/2, §26): 34 texts, 1531-1547.
- Imperial city of Biberach an der Riß (Sehling 17/2, §26): 3 texts, 1531-1548.
- Imperial city of Landau (Sehling 19/1, §28): 5 texts, 1526-1554.
- Imperial city of Worms (Sehling 19/1, §28): 3 texts, 1524-1543.
- Imperial city of Strasbourg (Sehling 20/1, §30): 46 texts, 1523-1548.
- Imperial city of Colmar (Sehling 20/2, §31): 12 texts, 1578-1615.
- Archbishopric of Cologne (Sehling 22, §33): 1 text, 1546.
- City of Münster (Sehling 22, §33): 4 texts, 1532-1533.

**Philippist (PH)**, 33 texts: churches that held to Melanchthon's later teaching and his
*Corpus doctrinae* of 1560, and would not accept the Formula of Concord. Most of them later
turned Reformed:
- the joint synods of the Hessian landgraviates (1568-1582), which took the Wittenberg *Corpus
  doctrinae* as their norm, and the Hessian orders that followed to 1598;
- Bremen, from Albert Hardenberg's dispute (1556) to the Reformed turn of 1580;
- Nassau-Dillenburg's Agende of 1575, which binds the pastors to the Augsburg Confession and the
  *Examen* of Philip Melanchthon;
- Anhalt under Prince Johann Georg before the Reformed turn of 1596.

- Principality of Anhalt (Sehling 2, §7): 2 texts, 1590-1594.
- City of Bremen (Sehling 7/2.2, §15): 9 texts, 1556-1575.
- The Hessian landgraviates: joint synods and orders (1567-1582) (Sehling 8, §16): 16 texts,
  1568-1582.
- Divided Hesse (1582-1618) (Sehling 9, §17): 5 texts, 1574-1598.
- Nassau-Dillenburg (Sehling 10, §18): 1 text, 1575.

### 3.4 Anglican (A)

One order in the set is Anglican: the "Old Discipline" and "New Discipline" of the English exile
congregation at Frankfurt (1557). It was founded in 1554 by Marian exiles under Whittingham and
John Knox. In 1555 a second group under Richard Cox, loyal to the Book of Common Prayer, took
the lead and drove Knox out. **Frankfurt, editor's introduction** (Sehling 9, p. 492):

<!-- doc 2331 -->
> Obwohl die englische Gemeinde nach Wegzug der Knox’schen Fraktion durchweg anglikanischen
> Charakter besaß, lässt die New Discipline innerhalb des bestehenden Rahmens des Second Book of
> Common Prayer Züge gemeindlicher Weiterentwicklung erkennen.

Although, after the departure of Knox's faction, the English congregation was thoroughly
Anglican in character, the New Discipline shows traits of congregational development within the
existing framework of the Second Book of Common Prayer.

- Imperial city of Frankfurt (Sehling 9, §17): 1 text, 1557.

Sehling calls the congregation "englisch-reformiert". It is coded A here because the editor
shows that its order was framed within the Prayer Book.

Two related orders are coded R:
- the order of John a Lasco's Dutch stranger church in London under Edward VI, in Micron's
  German abridgement (Sehling 7/1, p. 579);
- the French Reformed congregation's *Liturgia sacra* of 1554 at Frankfurt (Sehling 9, p. 562).
  Frankfurt also required the English congregation to use it.

### 3.5 Hussite: the Bohemian Brethren (H), and the Sandomierz consensus (C)

The Hussite tradition reaches the set through the Unity of the Brethren (Unitas Fratrum) in
Greater Poland. **Greater Poland, Sehling's introduction** (Sehling 4, p. 248):

<!-- doc 1845 -->
> In Polen verbreitete sich sowohl die lutherische als auch die reformirte Lehre gleichzeitig.
> Daneben entstanden Gemeinden der böhmischen Brüder in grösserer Zahl.

In Poland both the Lutheran and the Reformed teaching spread at the same time. Alongside them
arose congregations of the Bohemian Brethren in large numbers.

The Brethren had united with the Little Polish Reformed at Koźminek in 1555. In 1570, at
Sandomierz, they joined with the Reformed and the Lutherans in a common Consensus. Sehling
prints the Ostroróg privilege because of the town's place in the Unity. **Greater Poland,
Sehling's introduction** (Sehling 4, p. 254):

<!-- doc 1846 -->
> Da Ostrorog die Hauptgemeinde der Unität war und Sitz des Seniors, soll dieses Privileg nach
> einer mir gütigst zur Verfügung gestellten Übersetzung abgedruckt werden.

Since Ostroróg was the chief congregation of the Unity and the seat of the senior, this
privilege shall be printed from a translation kindly placed at my disposal.

The Brethren (H):
- Greater Poland (Posen): synods, the Bohemian Brethren and the towns (Sehling 4, §9): 3 texts,
  1569-1616.
  - Kirchenordnung der Böhmischen Brüder (1616; Sehling 4, p. 273).
  - Privileg für Lissa (1580; Sehling 4, p. 298).
  - Privileg für Ostrorog (1569; Sehling 4, p. 299).

The Sandomierz consensus and its general synods (C):
- Synode von Sendomir (1570; Sehling 4, p. 257).
- Synode von Posen (1570; Sehling 4, p. 258).
- Synode von Krakau (1573; Sehling 4, p. 260).
- Synode von Petrikau (1578; Sehling 4, p. 262).
- Synode von Wladislaw (1583; Sehling 4, p. 263).
- Synode von Thorn (1595; Sehling 4, p. 265).

The synods of Xions (1560) and Pińczów (1561) belong to the Little Polish Reformed church united
with the Brethren, and are coded R. The Lutheran synod of Gostyń (1565) and the town orders of
Fraustadt are coded L.

The Brethren's order of 1616 was adopted by their general synod at Zerawitz in Moravia; Sehling
prints it for the Polish congregations. No Utraquist order is in the set.

### 3.6 Others

- **Roman Catholic humanist, Erasmian reform (RCH).** The church orders of Duke Johann III of
  Jülich-Cleves-Berg (1532/33) and their successors. They reform the old church on humanist
  lines without leaving it (Sehling 21; §32.1).
- **Multi-confessional diet decrees (T).** The decrees of the Transylvanian diets (1552-1619)
  regulate a principality that received the Lutheran, Reformed, Unitarian and Catholic religions
  side by side. The Transylvanian Saxons' own orders are Lutheran. The *Consensus doctrinae* of
  1557 is also coded L (Sehling 24; §35).
- **Radical Reformation (RAD).** Thomas Müntzer's German church office, German evangelical Mass
  and order of the German service at Allstedt (1523/24). They are coded apart from the Lutheran
  orders around them in vol. 1.
- **Anabaptist (ANA).** The Münster council and guild masters' mandate of 31 January 1534, three
  weeks before the Anabaptist council was elected. It bound the citizens to let each keep his
  faith, and so recognized the Anabaptists as a religious group in the city
  (Sehling 22, p. 363). It is not an order made by Anabaptists: no Anabaptist church order is in
  the set.
- **Roman Catholic (RC).** Bishop Franz von Waldeck's edict of 3 February 1534 against the
  "verdampte ketterye unnde secte der wedderdoep" (Sehling 22, p. 364).
- **Groups with no order in the set.** There are no orders of the Waldensians (named only in
  passing, by Luther and in the editors' notes), the Lollards, the Utraquists, the
  Schwenckfelders, the Hutterites, the Mennonites or the Antitrinitarians. Mandates against
  Anabaptists, Schwenckfelders, Davidjorists, Mennonites and "Sakramentierer" are coded by the
  authority that issued them: Lutheran, Reformed, Moderate Reformed or, in Jülich, Roman
  Catholic humanist.
- **Treaty (R/L).** The East Frisian Delfzijl settlement of 1595 and the Concordats of 1599,
  between the Lutheran count and Reformed Emden.

---

## 4. The Interim orders

The Augsburg Interim of 1548 imposed a largely Catholic order on the Protestant estates until a
final council, allowing only the chalice and clerical marriage. The Leipzig Interim (1548/49)
was Electoral Saxony's milder version. As §2.2 shows, Sehling meant to print no Interim orders.
Those in the set fall into four groups:
- **Interim order**: an order made to put the Interim into effect, or to adapt a territory's
  order to it;
- **under the Interim**: a text issued while the Interim was in force, not itself an Interim
  order;
- **on the Interim**: a text about the Interim, such as a response, a settlement or a protest;
- **after the Interim** or **end of the Interim**: a text that ends or repairs it.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Several orders of the set are **described but not printed** because they are Interim orders:
- the Saxon (Leipzig) Interim agenda of 1549 (Sehling 1, p. 102; Sehling 2, pp. 501-505);
- the planned Hamburg Interim church order (Sehling 5, p. 484);
- the Brandenburg Interim order (Sehling 3, pp. 12, 177);
- the Göttingen "KO unter dem Interim" of 1550 (Sehling 6/2, p. 904);
- the Nuremberg Interim agenda (Sehling 11, pp. 328, 482);
- the Augsburg Interim forms (Sehling 12, p. 28);
- the draft of a Prussian church order "auf der Grundlage des Interims" (Sehling 4, p. 22).

Some cities kept Interim usages long after 1552. Schwäbisch Hall kept its Interim order until
1559 (Sehling 17/1, p. 34), and in Ansbach-Kulmbach the Mass vestment stayed "als Rest der
Interimszeit" (Sehling 11, p. 292).

### 29.2 Principality of Pfalz-Simmern

- **Tradition**: Lutheran; Reformed from 1598. Duke Reichard held to the Augsburg Confession
  until his death on 14 January 1598, when the principality fell to the Electoral Palatinate.
  The two mandates of 1598 were issued from Heidelberg under Elector Frederick IV, and the first
  ordered the churches cleared "im reformierten Sinne" (Sehling 19/2, p. 656).
